Weak lensing “post-Born” effects are equivalent to pure lens-lens couplings
Oliver Denton-Turner † and Eugene A. Lim †

Abstract
We show that the so-called “post-Born” effects of weak lensing at 4th order are equivalent to lens-lens couplings in the Born Approximation. We demonstrate this by explicitly showing the equivalence of the canonical weak lensing approach at 4th order using the anisotropy remapping method, to that of the 4th order calculation of the lens-lens coupling effects using the Boltzmann equation approach that was first developed in Su and Lim 2014 . Furthermore, we argue that to incorporate true “post-Born” effects, i.e. taking into account non-straight photon paths, require the addition of a photon deflection term which has not been taken into account in the canonical formalism nor the Boltzmann method.



KCL-PH-TH/2021-67

I
Introduction

Weak lensing of the cosmic microwave background (CMB) is now well established as an important probe of cosmological parameters
Pyne and Carroll 1996
;
Kaiser 1998
;
Wu et al. 2019
;
Namikawa et al. 2019
;
Aghanim et al. 2020
;
Blanchard and Schneider 1987
;
Zaldarriaga et al. 1997
;
Lewis 2005
;
Kaplinghat et al. 2003
;
Liu et al. 2016
;
Green et al. 2017
. By using the observations of the temperature
\Theta
, and the
E
and
B
polarisation anisotropies spectra of the CMB, we can reconstruct the underlying lensing potential
Bartelmann and Schneider 2001
;
Hanson et al. 2011
;
Lewis et al. 2011
;
Zaldarriaga and Seljak 1999
;
Hu 2001
;
Hanson et al. 2010
;
Peloton et al. 2017
;
Carron and Lewis 2017

\phi(\hat{{\bf n}})=-2\int_{0}^{r_{s}}dr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{s})\Psi_{W}
where
W(r_{1},r_{2})\equiv 1/r_{1}-1/r_{2}
is the lensing efficiency kernel
Bartelmann and Schneider 2001
;
Cooray and Hu 2002
,
\Psi_{W}
is the Weyl potential and
r_{s}
is the conformal distance to the last scattering surface (LSS). The lensing potential contains the projected information of the foreground distribution of matter, and hence the lensing potential allows us to probe its correlations
Aghanim et al. 2020
;
Song et al. 2003
;
Hand et al. 2015
;
Singh et al. 2016
;
Harnois-Déraps et al. 2017
;
Kirk et al. 2016
;
Liu and Hill 2015
;
Seljak and Zaldarriaga 1999
;
Goldberg and Spergel 1999
;
Namikawa 2016
;
Nguyen et al. 2019
;
Aguilar Faúndez et al. 2019
;
Das et al. 2013
;
Larsen and Challinor 2016
;
Schaan et al. 2017
;
Vallinotto 2012
.

To do so, a formalism to map the CMB anisotropies to
\phi(\hat{{\bf n}})
and implicitly
\Psi_{W}(r)
must be constructed. The standard canonical method focuses on the computation of the deflection angle
\alpha^{a}(\hat{{\bf n}})=\partial^{a}\phi(\hat{{\bf n}})
which can then be directly mapped order by order onto the observed
\Theta
spectra
Okamoto and Hu 2003
using the following
ansatz
, i.e.

\displaystyle\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}}+\hat{\alpha})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})+\alpha^{a}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{a}\alpha^{b}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})+\dots

(1)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\Theta^{[I]}(\hat{{\bf n}})+\Theta^{[II]}(\hat{{\bf n}})+\Theta^{[III]}(\hat{{\bf n}})+\dots.

This remapping can be carried out to increasingly higher orders in
\Theta

Krause and Hirata 2010
;
Cooray and Hu 2002
;
Pratten and Lewis 2016
;
Fanizza et al. 2015
;
Marozzi et al. 2016
.
While these effects are expected to be small, they may play a role in the estimates of systematics of higher order correlations
Fabbian et al. 2019
thus an accurate accounting of them is required.

In such calculations, the Born Approximation is applied – the line integral from the observer to the CMB source is assumed to be through the background unperturbed photon path
Lewis and Challinor 2006
. In the calculation of the 4th order lensing anisotropy, it is often suggested that the approximation no longer holds, and hence the physical effects are attributed to “post-Born” physics
Pratten and Lewis 2016
;
Marozzi et al. 2016
;
Marozzi et al. 2017
such as ray-deflection and time delay. In this paper, we argue that in such calculations, the Born Approximation still holds -- in other words the line integrals are still taken along the background unperturbed photon path
1
1

1

A numerical approach is undertaken by
Fabbian et al. 2018
which does take into account the ray deflection effects.
. Furthermore, we will show that these effects commonly attributed as “post-Born” are generated by higher order lens-lens couplings instead.

To show this, we will use an alternative method for calculating the effect of weak lensing on the CMB temperature anisotropies first introduced in
Su and Lim 2014
which is to directly solve the Boltzmann equation for the lensed anisotropies order by order. This is in contrast to solving the geodesic equation to obtain the deflection angle as an intermediary step, as in the case of the canonical method, and then mapping those onto the anistropies via Eqn.
1
. In
Su and Lim 2014
, the lensing anisotropies at 4th order due to pure lens-lens couplings were calculated, and in this paper, we will show that the results obtained in that paper is completely equivalent to that of the canonical method, and hence proving our assertion. Furthemore, we will identify the point of departure where true post-Born effects such as ray deflection should be introduced.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
II
we will describe the Boltzmann formalism as introduced in
Su and Lim 2014
, and identify the point where true post-Born effects could be introduced. In Section
III
, we will clarify the relationship between the the Boltzmann approach and the canonical approach, and explicitly prove the equivalence at 4th order, i.e. the results obtained in
Pratten and Lewis 2016
;
Marozzi et al. 2016
are completely equivalent to that first obtained in
Su and Lim 2014
. Finally in Section
IV
we conclude.

II
The Boltzmann Equation Approach

We now forego any
ansatz
and obtain all the higher order
\Theta
terms without any reference to the deflection angles by explicitly solving its Boltzmann evolution equation
Pitrou 2009
. This approach was introduced in
Su and Lim 2014
and used to calculate effects of lens-lens couplings on weak lensing to 4th order, and which this section closely follows.

The Boltzmann equation describes the evolution of a distribution of photons, i.e. the intensitiy
I
, as a function of spactime position
x^{\mu}
, frequency
p^{0}
and spatial direction
\hat{{\bf n}}

{\cal L}[I(x^{\mu},p^{0},\hat{{\bf n}})]=2{\cal C}(x^{\mu},p^{0},\hat{{\bf n}})~,

(2)

where
{\cal L}
is the Liouville operator which defines its free evolution
{\cal L}=\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}\eta
, with
\eta
being the conformal time. It is a first order
total derivative
, encapsulating much of the physics of the evolution despite its notational simplicity. Meanwhile,
{\cal C}=-1/2\tau^{\prime}I+{\cal D}
is the collision term where
\tau^{\prime}=\partial_{\eta}\tau
is the time derivative of the optical depth and
{\cal D}
is the Compton scattering term.

The temperature anisotropy
\Theta
is directly related to the energy averaged intensity
\hat{I}
and hence
I
by the following relation

\Theta=\frac{1}{4}\hat{I}~,~\hat{I}(x^{\mu},\hat{{\bf n}})=\frac{\int I(x^{\mu},p^{0},\hat{{\bf n}})(p^{0})^{3}dp^{0}}{\int{\cal I}(p^{0})(p^{0})^{3}dp^{0}}~,

(3)

where
{\cal I}
is the isotropic background blackbody spectrum. The Boltzmann equation serves as the evolution equation for
\Theta
, thus we can expand
\hat{I}
as usual
\hat{I}=\sum_{N}\hat{I}^{[N]}/N!
which is related order by order to the expansion
\Theta=\sum_{N}\Theta^{[N]}
by
\Theta^{[N]}=\hat{I}^{[N]}/4N!
.

Expanding the Liouville term
{\cal L}
,

\displaystyle{\cal L}=\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}+{\mathrm{d}x^{a}\over\mathrm{d}\eta}D_{a}+{\mathrm{d}p^{0}\over\mathrm{d}\eta}{\partial\over\partial p^{0}}+{\mathrm{d}n^{a}\over\mathrm{d}\eta}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\right],

(4)

where
D_{a}
is the spatial covariant derivative along the photon trajectory
x^{a}
and
p^{a}\equiv\mathrm{d}x^{a}/\mathrm{d}\eta
is its first derivative. The first term is the overall time evolution of the intensity
\partial_{\eta}I
and the third term denotes the redshifting of the photon energy – for now we will only keep this term at 1st order in perturbation (as shown in
Su and Lim 2014
, higher order terms are subdominant).

The fourth term encodes the weak lensing effect – at first order, it is given by the familiar spatial geodesic equation
(\mathrm{d}p^{a}/\mathrm{d}\eta)^{[I]}=-2\delta^{ab}\partial_{b}\Psi_{W}
. However, since the intensity is a function of
p^{a}
,
we do not need to directly integrate this equation
-- the physics of geodesic deviation is automatically folded into the formalism. Indeed since we assume that there is no backreaction of the perturbed photon intensity to the metric
2
2

2

To incorporate this effect, we would need to solve the perturbed Einstein equation to obtain the 2nd order metric perturbations. This effect is also ignored in the standard canonical formalism.
, the fourth term of Eqn.
4

to all orders
is
Su and Lim 2014

\frac{\mathrm{d}n^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=-2S^{ab}\partial_{b}\Psi_{W}~,

(5)

where
S_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}-n_{\mu}n_{\nu}
projects onto the hypersurfaces labeled by the cosmic time
\eta
such that the co-moving observer measures the photons at energy
p^{0}
. We will assume that the spatial components
S^{ab}\rightarrow\delta^{ab}
to match to the fact that in the remapping ansatz,
\hat{{\bf n}}+\hat{\bf\alpha}
is assumed to be a unit vector (even though in principle it is not).

Finally, the second term
\mathrm{d}x^{a}/\mathrm{d}\eta
captures deviation of the photon trajectory from the unperturbed background path
\bar{\bf x}
. A hint can be gleaned by considering the first order term of its coefficient

\left(\frac{dx^{a}}{d\eta}\right)^{[I]}=2n^{a}\Psi_{W}~,

(6)

i.e. it depends on
\Psi_{W}
and
not its spatial derivative
, unlike the lensing term. This fact is true to all orders in perturbation theory. If we ignore all the other interactions of the Liouville operator and focus on this term and the evolution term, at first order it is

\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}I=\frac{dx^{a}}{d\eta}D_{a}I~,

(7)

which can be written as

\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}-2\Psi_{W}n^{a}D_{a}\right)I=0~,

(8)

describing an integral along the deflected photon path. Simply put, Eqn.
6
is an equation of motion for
{\bf x}
as a function of
\Psi({{\bf x}})
thus a full accounting of it will encode the effects of deviating from the background path
\bar{{\bf x}}
. An equivalent interpretation of Eqn.
6
is that since the RHS depends purely on
\Psi_{W}
and not its spatial derivative, it captures the
time delay
of the photon path due to the presence of perturbations
Fanizza et al. 2018
.

In principle, this term should be considered – it represents “post-Born” effects in the strictest sense as including this term would mean that
\Psi_{W}
is now a function of
{\bf x}
(and not just
\bar{{\bf x}}
), capturing deviations from the unperturbed path. This term is
not
captured by the canonical formalism since
all
quantities in this formalism is evaluated at the background path
\bar{{\bf x}}
– note that the photon path perturbation
\delta{\bf x}
itself is derived by solving the geodesic equation order by order using the Weyl potential evaluated at
\bar{{\bf x}}
. As we will see below, by dropping this term, we will recover an equivalent relationship between the Boltzmann approach and the remapping ansatz.

Given these approximations, and integrating over the energy Eqn.
3
, the Boltzmann equation for
\Theta
to
N
-th order is
3
3

3

For a detailed derivation, see
Su and Lim 2014
.

\left[\partial_{\eta}+n^{a}\partial_{a}+\tau^{\prime}\right]\Theta^{[N]}=\hat{{\cal D}}^{[N]}-2\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\Psi_{W}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta^{[N-1]}~.

(9)

where
\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\equiv\delta^{ab}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}
. Focusing on lensing, we will ignore Compton scattering terms beyond first order, i.e.
\hat{{\cal D}}^{[N]}=0
for
N>1
. This hierarchy of equations can be solved iteratively using standard perturbation theory as follows.

Transitioning to Fourier space, the first order solution is obtained from the familiar line-of-sight integral
Seljak and Zaldarriaga 1996

\displaystyle\Theta^{[I]}(\eta,{\bf{k}},\hat{\bf{n}})=e^{\tau(\eta)}\int^{\eta}_{0}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}~S_{T}e^{i{{\bf k}\cdot\hat{{\bf n}}}(\tilde{\eta}-\eta)}~,

(10)

where the source function
S_{T}(\tilde{\eta},\Psi_{W},{\bf k}\cdot\hat{{\bf n}}/k)
describes the usual 1st order collision term physics
Dodelson 2003
, and
\eta=0
is the time of mode reentry.

At
N>1
order, the Boltzmann equation Eqn.
9
can be directly integrated in Fourier space using the standard line-of-sight integral to obtain the rather intimidating recursive equation

\displaystyle e^{-i{\bf k}_{N+1}\cdot\hat{{\bf n}}r_{N+1}-\bar{\tau}(\eta_{N+1})}\Theta^{[N+1]}(\eta_{N+1},{\bf k}_{N+1},\hat{{\bf n}})=\int_{0}^{\eta_{N+1}}d\eta_{N}\int\frac{d{\bf k}_{N}^{\prime}d{\bf k}_{N}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\delta({\bf k}_{N+1}-{\bf k}_{N}-{\bf k}_{N}^{\prime})

(11)

\displaystyle\times\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\left(e^{-i{\bf k}^{\prime}_{N}\cdot\hat{{\bf n}}r_{N}}\Psi_{W}({\bf k}_{N}^{\prime})\right)\underbrace{\left[-\left(\frac{2}{r_{N}}\right)\left(\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}+ik_{N,a}r_{N}\right)\right]}_{(\hat{\Box}_{\hat{{\bf n}},r_{N}})_{a}}\left(e^{-i{\bf k}_{N}\cdot\hat{{\bf n}}r_{N}-\bar{\tau}(\eta_{N})}\Theta^{[N]}({\bf k}_{N},\eta_{N},\hat{{\bf n}})\right)~,

(12)

where
\bar{\tau}(\eta_{N})\equiv\int_{\eta_{N}}^{\eta_{0}}\tau^{\prime}(\bar{\eta})d\bar{\eta}
and

r_{N}\equiv\eta_{0}-\eta_{N}
, with
\eta_{0}
being the conformal time today, and
\bar{\bf x}=\hat{{\bf n}}r
. To make contact with the notation in the remapping approach, we define
r_{s}=\eta_{0}-\eta_{LSS}
, where
\eta_{LSS}
is the time of recombination.

The key operator in the above equation is the underbraced
lensing operator

(\hat{\Box}_{\hat{{\bf n}},r})_{a}
, which generates a lensing kernel
W(r,\tilde{r})
when acting on a source or lensing potential,

\int\frac{d^{3}{\bf k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}(\hat{\Box}_{\hat{{\bf n}},\tilde{r}})_{a}\left(e^{-i{\bf k}\cdot\hat{{\bf n}}r}\Psi_{W}({\bf k})\right)=-2W(r,\tilde{r})r\partial_{a}\Psi_{W}({\bar{{\bf x}}})~.

(13)

Eqn.
13
is simply the integrand of the 1st order deflection angle when solving the geodesic equation (see Eqn.
17
in the next section), justifying its moniker. In other words,
(\hat{\Box}_{\hat{{\bf n}},\tilde{r}})_{a}\chi(r{\hat{{\bf n}}})
describes the lensing action of a lens at
\tilde{r}
on an object
\chi
at
r
, which can be a lens
\partial^{a}\Psi_{W}
or the source itself
\Theta^{[I]}
.

Crucially, the
N
-th order lensing operator acts linearly on
\Theta^{[N]}
, which is a nested integral of
\Theta^{[N-1]}
,
\Theta^{[N-2]}
etc, until it terminates at
\Theta^{[I]}
which itself is an integral over the source
S_{T}
(e.g. Eqn.
10
). At each order, Eqn.
12
tells us that each nested integral includes a Weyl term
\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\Psi_{W}(\bf x)
– this is simply a lens at location
{\bf x}
. Thus
the
N
-th order lensing operator acts on all lenses associated with terms of order
N-1
and below
, until it terminates with a lensing of the source itself. The linearity of the operator allows us to use the product rule to generate all possible lensing terms, with the proper time ordering automatically enforced by the nested integral structure – a near lens will lense all far lenses and the source, but not
vice versa
. At
N
-th order,
\Theta^{[N]}
will have
(N-1)!
distinct terms.

Schematically,

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[N+1]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{\eta_{0}}_{\eta_{s}}d\eta_{N}\hskip 2.84526pt{\nabla}^{a_{N}}_{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}\left[{\Psi}_{W}(r_{N})\right]({\hat{\Box}}_{\hat{\mathbf{n}},r_{N}})_{a_{N}}\bigg\{...

(14)

\displaystyle\int^{\eta_{3}}_{\eta_{s}}d\eta_{2}{\nabla}^{a_{2}}_{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}\left[{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\right]({\hat{\Box}}_{\hat{\mathbf{n}},r_{2}})_{a_{2}}\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{\eta_{2}}_{\eta_{s}}d\eta_{1}{\nabla}^{a_{1}}_{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}\left[{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\right]({\hat{\Box}}_{\hat{\mathbf{n}},r_{1}})_{a_{1}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}...\bigg\}.

As shown in
Su and Lim 2014
, ignoring the action of the lensing operator
(\hat{\Box}_{\hat{{\bf n}},r})_{a}
on all
\Psi_{W}
terms except the innermost term in the nested integral is equivalent to ignoring all lens-lens couplings. Restoring lens-lens couplings, we will now show that
all
the terms of the remapping
ansatz
are generated.

III
Equivalence of the Remapping and Boltzmann Methods

The remapping approach
Lewis and Challinor 2006
begins with the following
ansatz
, where the lensed temperature anisotropy
\tilde{\Theta}(\hat{{\bf n}})\equiv\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}}+\hat{\alpha})
is expressed as a Taylor expansion of its unlensed counterpart
\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})
, in terms of the
total deflection angle

\alpha^{a}=\alpha^{a}{}^{[I]}+\alpha^{a}{}^{[II]}+\dots
, with the observer plane at
r=0
,

\displaystyle\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}}+\hat{\alpha})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})+\alpha^{a}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{a}\alpha^{b}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})+\dots

(15)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\Theta^{[I]}(\hat{{\bf n}})+\Theta^{[II]}(\hat{{\bf n}})+\Theta^{[III]}(\hat{{\bf n}})+\dots.

where
\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\equiv\partial/\partial n^{a}
and the index
a=1,2,3
runs over the spatial components of the coordinate basis. These terms are identified, order by order, to the corresponding higher order temperature anisotropies
\Theta^{(N)}(\hat{{\bf n}})
.

The intervening metric perturbations – namely the Weyl potential
\Psi_{W}
– between the source plane at
r_{s}
and the observer plane at
r=0
are encoded in the deflection
\delta{\bf x}
around the unlensed path
\bar{{\bf x}}
, which is obtained by solving the geodesic equation

\frac{\partial^{2}\delta x^{a}}{\partial r^{2}}=-2\partial^{a}\Psi_{W}(\bar{{\bf x}}+\delta{\bf x})~,

(16)

where
\partial^{a}\equiv\delta^{ab}\partial/\partial x^{b}
. To find the deflection at each order, we solve Eqn.
16
order by order by expanding
\delta{\bf x}=\delta{\bf x}^{[I]}+\delta{\bf x}^{[II]}+\dots
, and the Weyl potential
\Psi_{W}({\bar{{\bf x}}}+\delta{\bf x})=\Psi_{W}(\bar{{\bf x}})+\partial_{a}\Psi_{W}(\bar{{\bf x}})\delta x^{a}+(1/2)\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\Psi_{W}(\bar{{\bf x}})\delta x^{a}\delta x^{b}+\dots
.
At first order, using the standard Green’s function technique, the solution is

\delta x^{a}{}^{[I]}(r\hat{{\bf n}})=-2r\int_{0}^{r}\mathrm{d}r_{1}~W(r_{1},r)r_{1}\partial^{a}\Psi_{W}(r_{1}\hat{{\bf n}})~,

(17)

recalling that
r\hat{{\bf n}}=\bar{{\bf x}}
. Henceforth, we drop
\hat{{\bf n}}
from the argument for simplicity – it is implicitly assumed that all integrands are evaluated at the background path
\bar{{\bf x}}
.
The deflection angle is given by the small angle formula

\alpha^{a}{}^{[N]}(r)=\frac{\delta x^{a}{}^{[N]}(r)}{r}~.

(18)

Using the remapping series expansion (
15
), the first order temperature anisotropies,
{\Theta}^{[I]}
, are unlensed. Lensing appears at second order in the expansion

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[II]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\alpha}^{a[I]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

(19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\mathrm{d}r_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}}),

where we have defined the position of the source to be
r_{s}
, the conformal distance from the observer to last scattering surface of the CMB. It can be seen from the form of the integral, which is first order in
\Psi_{W}
that this represents the anisotropies being lensed a single time between the source and the observer.
This methodology is standard
Lewis and Challinor 2006
, and terms up to
\alpha^{[III]}
and
\Theta^{[IV]}
have been calculated in the literature
Pratten and Lewis 2016
;
Marozzi et al. 2016
;
Fabbian et al. 2018
;
Krause and Hirata 2010
;
Calabrese et al. 2015
.
In this work, we introduce a diagrammatic approach (detailed in Appendix
A
) in order to make the time ordering structure of the solutions manifest
4
4

4

Note that this diagrammatic formalism is not equivalent to the diagrammatic method introduced for the Boltzmann method in
Su and Lim 2014
.
. Using Eqn.
17
, we define

\delta x^{a}{}^{[I]}(r)\equiv\hbox to42.15pt{\vbox to22.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.72769pt\lower-12.67155pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

(20)

where we diagrammatically illustrate that the source at
r
is being lensed by a lens at
r_{1}
– setting
r
equal to
r_{s}
recovers the first order deflection. Operationally, one can read the dashed line as an integral over the kernel
-2\int_{0}^{r}dr_{1}W(r,r_{1})r_{1}\partial^{a}\chi(r_{1})
acting on
\chi(r_{1})
which is one of the terms of the Taylor expansion of
\Psi_{W}(\bar{x}^{a}+\delta x^{a})
i.e
\Psi_{W}(r)
,
\partial_{b}\Psi_{W}\delta x^{b}
and
(1/2)\partial_{b}\partial_{c}\Psi_{W}\delta x^{b}\delta x^{c}+\dots
etc.
Crucially, the arguments of the kernel
W(r,r_{1})
must be the upper limit of the integral
r
and the position
r_{1}
of
\chi(r_{1})
which is integrated over.
This is a direct consequence of solving the geodesic equation using the Green’s function. Since the geodesic equation is a 2nd order linear evolution equation, standard perturbation theory tells us that the order
N
-th term is a nested time integral over all the possible permutations of lower order terms which summed to
N-1
. This nested time integrals encodes a strict time ordering as follows. At second order (see e.g.
Marozzi et al. 2016
)

\displaystyle\delta x^{a}{}^{[II]}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\int^{r}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{1},r)r_{1}\partial^{a}\partial_{b}\Psi_{W}(r_{1})\underbrace{\delta x^{b}{}^{[I]}(r_{1})}_{\hbox to28.62pt{\vbox to14.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.77223pt\lower-8.88011pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\displaystyle=

r

which intuitively shows that the lens at
r_{2}
lenses a lens at
r_{1}
which itself lenses the source (or possibly another lens) at
r
. We can use these archetypes to construct higher order terms iteratively, for example at third order

(22)

where in the last line we have combined the diagrams to make the integral structure manifest – lenses at
r_{2}
and
r_{3}
lense the lens at
r_{1}
, which itself lenses the source at
r
. Note that the term is symmetric under
r_{2}\leftrightarrow r_{3}
.

We can use these archetypes to calculate individual contributions to
\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})^{[N]}
. The third order anisotropies are given by

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[III]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})=\alpha^{a[II]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})+{1\over 2}\alpha^{a[I]}\alpha^{b[I]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

(23)

The first term is given by Eqn.
21
, whilst the second is the product of a pair of
\delta x^{a}{}^{[I]}
terms, so that we have the following pair of diagrams

The third order anisotropies are then

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[III]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\left[\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\right]\hskip-2.84526pt\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip 1.42262pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}}).

The second order
{\cal O}(\Psi_{W})^{2}
terms evidence the fact that this represents two lensing events between the source and the observer. The second term simply represents the anisotropies being lensed a pair of times, whereas the first term is due to our earlier perturbative expansions of the Weyl potential and transverse deflection. It represents lens-lens coupling, i.e. the distortion of the first lens by the second.

At 4th order,
\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})^{[IV]}
must contain various permutations of
\alpha^{a}{}^{[I]}
,
\alpha^{a}{}^{[II]}
and
\alpha^{a}{}^{[III]}
terms
Pratten and Lewis 2016
;
Marozzi et al. 2016

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[IV]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})={\alpha}^{a[III]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})+{1\over 2}\left({\alpha}^{a[I]}{\alpha}^{b[II]}+{\alpha}^{a[II]}{\alpha}^{b[I]}\right)\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})+{1\over 3!}{\alpha}^{a[I]}{\alpha}^{b[I]}{\alpha}^{c[I]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})~.

These terms are well known in the literature (e.g.
Pratten and Lewis 2016
;
Marozzi et al. 2016
;
Krause and Hirata 2010
) – in terms of our diagrammatic formalism they are as follows.

The first term of Eqn.
is directly obtained from Eqn.
22
and Eqn.
18
, with
r=r_{s}
and noting that the
\partial_{a}\Theta(\hat{{\bf n}})
term can be brought into the integral since it is not a function of time. This gives us two total terms which are

\displaystyle\Theta^{IV}

\displaystyle\supset\frac{\delta x^{a}{}^{[III]}}{r}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta=\hbox to64.91pt{\vbox to18.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.72769pt\lower-12.67155pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

Physically this captures the lensing of the source by a lens at
r_{1}
, while the lenses at
r_{3}
lenses another lens at
r_{2}
which then lenses the source.
Its symmetric counterpart
\alpha^{a[II]}\alpha^{b[I]}
is obtained under the permutation
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{3}
,
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}
and
r_{3}\rightarrow r_{2}
.
The third term in our diagrammatic method is

\displaystyle r_{s}^{3}{\alpha}^{a[I]}{\alpha}^{b[I]}{\alpha}^{c[I]}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 9.21 15.96 38.03 15.96 47.24 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~,

with the physical interpretation that the source at
r_{s}
is lensed separately by lenses at
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
and
r_{3}
– i.e. this is the sole term if we ignore all lens-lens couplings.
As we have mentioned in section
II
, while these terms are often called “post-Born” in the literature (e.g.
Petri et al. 2017
;
Barthelemy et al. 2020
), the Weyl potentials
\Psi_{W}
in the integrands are evaluated along the unperturbed path
\bar{{\bf x}}
, and hence morally speaking we are still applying the Born Approximation.

Summing these terms together, at fourth order the anisotropies are

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[IV]}_{remapping}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})=A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}+C^{\prime}+D^{\prime}+F^{\prime},

(31)

where

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{2},r_{1})

(32)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-4.2679ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\big[\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\big]\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\hskip-2.84526pt\Bigg\},

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526pt-\Bigg\{\hskip-1.42262pt4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\big[\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\big]

(33)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle C^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{4\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\bigg]\hskip-2.84526pt\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})

(34)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\bigg]\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle D^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{4\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg]

(35)

\displaystyle\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\bigg]\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle F^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{{4\over 3}\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\bigg]\hskip-2.84526pt\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg]

(36)

\displaystyle\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\bigg]\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\}.

Note that there is no term
E^{\prime}
as an additional term
E
initially appears to be unique to the Boltzmann equation solution as we will see later; the terms are ordered by the number of couplings to the source. At
\Theta^{[IV]}(\hat{{\bf n}})
order, there are a total of 5 terms, although the symmetric Eqn.
III
terms are counted twice
Krause and Hirata 2010
. The number of terms, including symmetric ones, in such an expansion is given by the Catalan number
C_{N-1}
with
C_{N}=\frac{1}{N+1}\binom{2N}{N}
. So at
\Theta^{[V]}
order, there are 14 terms etc. We prove this in Appendix
A
.

III.1
Equivalence at Third Order

As a warm up to the fourth order problem, we will now prove the equivalence of the Boltzmann method with the canonical remapping method at third order. Using the Boltzmann formalism, we find at this order

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[III]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}.

As we will prove below, this equation Eqn.
III.1
is completely equivalent to Eqn.
III
derived using the canonical method; it represents two lensing events between the source and the observer. The second term represents the anisotropies being lensed a pair of times, the first represents lens-lens coupling. In comparison with the geodesic equation method, we did not have to relax the Born approximation to include “ray-deflection”terms to produce this result. The above formula is due to gravitational lensing only, with no redshift, time-delay or ray-deflection.
That we have been able to derive Eqn.
III
implies that the the ray-deflection terms included in previous work
Shapiro and Cooray 2006
Pratten and Lewis 2016
are in fact also due to lens-lens coupling. In contrast with the previous method, solving for each order in a consistent way, rather than dealing with multiple Taylor expansions, has resulted in a single integration path for both terms.

We will now show that Eqn.
III.1
is equivalent to Eqn.
III
by first rearranging the order of the integration limits using the formula

\displaystyle\int^{r_{M+1}}_{0}dr_{M}\int^{r_{M}}_{0}d\tilde{r}=\int^{r_{M+1}}_{0}d\tilde{r}\int^{r_{M+1}}_{\tilde{r}}dr_{M}

(38)

to reverse the integration path of Eqn.
III.1
and obtain

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[III]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\bigg\{

(39)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{1},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{s},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}.

The second term has a symmetry that allows the integrals to be decoupled using the relation

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}f(r_{1})\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}f(r_{2})\cdots\int^{r_{N-1}}_{0}dr_{N}f(r_{N})={1\over N!}\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}f(r_{1})\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{2}f(r_{2})\cdots\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{N}f(r_{N})

(40)

with the result being

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[III]}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\bigg\{

(41)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{1},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{s},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

which matches the remapping method result, Eqn.
III
. The reason for the differing integration paths in results derived using the remapping method is now clear: it is because the nested integrals had already been decoupled. This is due to the multiplication of
\alpha^{a}
terms in Eqn.
15
. The coupling between the integrals represented by each
\alpha^{a}
is already broken in the remapping series.

III.2
Equivalence at Fourth Order

The solution from the Boltzmann equation for the fourth order anisotropies is given by the sum of the six terms, which was first derived in
Su and Lim 2014
,

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[IV]}_{Boltzmann}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})=A+B+C+D+E+F,

(42)

which have the form

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})]\Bigg\{\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}W(r_{2},r_{3})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(43)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(44)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{3})W(r_{1},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\{\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}W(r_{2},r_{3})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(45)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\{\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(46)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{3})W(r_{1},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(47)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{3})W(r_{s},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(48)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-8.53581ptdr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{3})W(r_{s},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}.

As with Eqn.
31
, this represents three lensing events between the source and the observer. The final term represents the anisotropies being lensed a trio of times, the other terms represent various combinations of lens-lens coupling. Due to the systematic nature of this derivation we once again have a single integration path for all six terms. Unlike Eqn.
31
, we now have six terms, whereas the geodesic equation method yielded only five. However, as we will show below, similar to the 3rd order case, the complicated integral structure of the two sets of terms hide their equivalence despite the differing number of terms. In other words, in comparison to the 5 terms of the canonical remapping method Eqn.
31
, the above terms are completely equivalent:
A=A^{\prime}
,
B=B^{\prime}
,
F=F^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}+D^{\prime}=C+D+E
.

Working through Eqn.
42
term by term, we begin with the first term, term
A
:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})]\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}W(r_{2},r_{3})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

and as with the third order result we use Eqn.
38
to rearrange the order of integration to obtain

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{1},r_{2})\int^{r_{2}}_{0}dr_{3}W(r_{2},r_{3})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

matching the equivalent term Eqn.
32
which was one of the two terms derived from the term
{\alpha}^{a[III]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})
in the series expansion Eqn.
. This had the diagrammatic form:

r
r_{1}
r_{2}
r_{3}
.
We next move on to term
B
which also has only a single coupling to the source

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(51)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{3})W(r_{1},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

rearranging the order of integration to obtain

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\Bigg\{

(52)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{1},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}dr_{3}W(r_{1},r_{3})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\}.

The inner pair of integrals again have a symmetry that allows us to use Eqn.
40
to decouple them, giving

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\Bigg\{

(53)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{1},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{3}W(r_{1},r_{3})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

so that this term also now matches the equivalent term Eqn.
33
, the second of the two terms derived from the term
{\alpha}^{a[III]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})
in the series expansion Eqn.
and has the diagrammatic form

r
r_{1}
r_{2}
r_{3}
. Moving on to term
C
:

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\{\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}W(r_{2},r_{3})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(54)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

we once more rearrange the order of integration to obtain

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\Bigg\{

(55)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{s},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}dr_{3}W(r_{2},r_{3})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\}.

We repeat this process with the fourth term,
D
;

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\{\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(56)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{3})W(r_{1},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

rearranging the order of integration

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\Bigg\{

(57)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{1},r_{2})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{3}W(r_{s},r_{3})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

to obtain the final Boltzmann equation method result for this term.
We now focus on the fifth term, which we label term
E
:

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(58)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{1}W(r_{1},r_{3})W(r_{s},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{s},r_{2})\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{3}W(r_{1},r_{3})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

where we have rearranged the order of the integration as usual in the 2nd line. The sixth term, which we shall label term
F
, is

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}dr_{2}\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

(59)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-8.53581ptdr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{3})W(r_{s},r_{2})W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{s},r_{2})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-8.53581ptdr_{3}W(r_{s},r_{3})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

which again we rearranged the order of the integration in the 2nd line.
This has a symmetry that allows it to be decoupled using Eqn.
40

after which it takes the form

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{4\over 3}\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{1}W(r_{s},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\Bigg\{

(60)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{0}dr_{2}W(r_{s},r_{2})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg\{

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-8.53581ptdr_{3}W(r_{s},r_{3})\hskip 2.84526pt\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\bigg\}\Bigg\},

which matches the result for the term Eqn.
36
produced by the product of three deflection angles;
{1\over 6}{\alpha}^{a[I]}{\alpha}^{b[I]}{\alpha}^{c[I]}\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})
and has the diagrammatic form

r_{s}
r_{1}
r_{2}
r_{3}
.

After these rearrangements the solution from the Boltzmann equation for the
\Theta^{(IV)}
is now given by the sum of the six terms:

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{[IV]}_{Boltzmann}(\hat{\mathbf{n}})=A+B+C+D+E+F,

(61)

where

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})

(62)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\big[\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\big]\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\hskip-1.42262pt\Bigg\},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526pt-\Bigg\{4\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 1.42262ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\big[\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\big]

(63)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})

(64)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(65)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})

(66)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\nabla_{a}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{{4\over 3}\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\bigg]\hskip-2.84526pt\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\bigg]

(67)

\displaystyle\bigg[\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-5.69054ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\bigg]\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}\Theta(\hat{\mathbf{n}})\Bigg\}.

By comparing these solutions term by term to the remapping solution, Eqn.
31
, one can see that three of the terms coincide;
A=A^{\prime}
,
B=B^{\prime}
and
F=F^{\prime}
. However, as previously noted, terms
C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
are symmetric in the remapping solution, and are represented by the diagram

r_{s}
r_{1}
r_{2}
r_{3}

and its symmetric counterpart, whereas in the Boltzmann solution terms
C
and
D
differ due to the time ordering information encoded in the nested integrals. As
C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
are symmetric they can be added together to form a single term:

\displaystyle C^{\prime}+D^{\prime}\equiv G=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(68)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\},

where we have simplified the expression by dropping the derivatives of the first order anisotropies (we are working with the expression for the deflection angle product:
{\alpha}^{a[I]}{\alpha}^{b[II]}
).

To prove the equivalence of the Boltzmann equation and remapping methods we must show that this term
G
is equivalent to the sum of the terms
C
,
D
and

, which written in this simplified form are

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(69)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(70)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\},

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(71)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\}.

By examination we can see that term

is most similar in form to

and we can in fact immediately recover it by splitting the third integral in

:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(72)

\displaystyle\bigg[\underbrace{\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})}_{1}+\underbrace{\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})}_{2}\bigg]\Bigg\},

where we have split

\displaystyle G1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\},~\mathrm{and}~

\displaystyle G2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\}.

We see that

. Focusing on

\displaystyle G2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\},

where it can be seen that

. We now have one term left from the Boltzmann equation solution to recover, term
C
, and two terms from the remapping method from which to do so,
G2b
and

.

can be brought closer to the form of

by rearranging the order of integration using Eqn.

so that

\displaystyle G2a2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})

(84)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\}.

Exchanging the labels of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
then gives the correct form of the lensing effiency function

\displaystyle G2a2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(85)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\}.

Finally, the we split the third integral

\displaystyle G2a2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(86)

\displaystyle\bigg[\underbrace{\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})}_{a}-\underbrace{\int^{r_{2}}_{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})}_{b}\bigg]\Bigg\},

to obtain two terms

\displaystyle G2a2a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(87)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\},~\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle G2a2b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(88)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\}.

The first term is in fact the final recovered term,
G2a2a=C
. We have recovered all of the necessary terms, but are left with two residual terms,
G2b
and
G2a2b
which must cancel to prove equivalence between our two results:

\displaystyle G2b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})

(89)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\Bigg\},~\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle G2a2b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{-8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{1}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})

(90)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{1}}_{r_{2}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{2})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\}.

We begin bringing them to the same form by rearranging the order of integration of
G2b
using Eqn.
38

\displaystyle G2b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{3}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})

(91)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{s}}_{r_{3}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\Bigg\},

then repeating this operation to bring the third integral out to the front:

\displaystyle G2b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})

(92)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{3}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\Bigg\}.

Turning to term
G2a2b
, we exchange the labels
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
:

\displaystyle G2a2b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{-8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})

(93)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{2}}_{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})\Bigg\},

then rearrange the order of integration to obtain

\displaystyle G2a2b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{-8\int^{r_{s}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{2}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{2},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{b}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{2})\hskip-2.84526pt\int^{r_{2}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{3}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{3},r_{1})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{c}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{3})

(94)

\displaystyle\int^{r_{3}}_{0}\hskip-2.84526ptdr_{1}\hskip 2.84526ptW(r_{1},r_{s})\nabla_{\hat{{\bf n}}}^{a}\nabla_{c}^{\hat{{\bf n}}}{\Psi}_{W}(r_{1})\Bigg\}.

It can then be seen that
G2b+G2a2b=0
, proving that the Boltzmann equation and the remapping method are equivalent to fourth order.

IV
Discussion and Conclusion

In this paper, we prove the equivalence of the canonical weak lensing remapping ansatz and the Boltzmann approach, up to 4th order. We believe that this equivalence is general to all orders, although a general proof eludes us at this moment. The use of the Boltzmann approach allows us to shed insight into the so-called “post-Born” effects of weak lensing, allowing us to demonstrate that the such effects are simply lens-lens coupling, and the Born Approximation is still valid – all the line integrals are performed along the unperturbed photon path
\bar{{\bf x}}
. The Boltzmann approach also identifies the exact point where true post-Born effects could be incorporated – this lies in the 2nd term of the Liouville expansion Eqn.
4
which is presently neglected. As argued in
Su and Lim 2014
we expect this term to be non-dominant.

Ultimately, whether or not to call higher order weak lensing effects “post-Born” is a question of nomenclature. As discussed in the literature
Beck et al. 2018
;
Pratten and Lewis 2016
;
Marozzi et al. 2017
;
Marozzi et al. 2016
;
Fabbian et al. 2018
;
Takahashi et al. 2017
;
Fabbian et al. 2019
, 4th order lensing effects on the CMB is very small. A full treatment
ala
Boltzmann has shown that this effect is
\sim 0.01\%
of the CMB at the most
Su and Lim 2014
, thus is negligible except perhaps as a systematic. On the other hand, in studies of weak lensing of the galaxy power at higher orders follow very similar “remapping” approaches
Krause and Hirata 2010
– instead of the unlensed CMB as the source, we replace the
\Theta^{[I]}
power spectrum with the equivalent unlensed galaxy power spectrum. Up to 4th order, the effects are estimated at
1-5\sigma
level
Krause and Hirata 2010
;
Petri et al. 2017
and hence a full accounting of all the lensing terms is potentially important in future studies
Böhm et al. 2020
.

Acknowledgements.
We would like to thank Tasos Avgoustidis, Alex Jenkins, David Mulryne, and Simon Su for their help and comments on this work. EAL was supported an STFC AGP grant no. ST/L000717/1 for part of this work.

Appendix A
Diagrammatic Representation for Remapping Ansatz

In this appendix, we describe the diagrammatic rules for the construction of deflection integrals to all orders following the remapping
ansatz
, and use this formalism to prove that the number of diagrams at
N
-th order is given by the Catalan number. We will drop
\hat{{\bf n}}
from the following for notational simplicity. In this section, “order
N
” refers to the order of the deflection
\delta x^{a}{}^{[N]}
. We emphasise that this representation is different to the diagrammatic representation for the Boltzmann formalism we introduced in
Su and Lim 2014
.

The basic archetype is the dashed line connecting a left node associated with position
r
to a right node with position
r_{1}
which corresponds to an
\int dr_{1}
integral over the kernel
W(r,r_{1})

(95)

where
M^{a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}}
is an object with
i
indices constructed out of products of
\delta x^{a}
.
\chi(r_{1})
is an unindexed object which is a full contraction of
M^{a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}}
with the
i
-th order term of the Taylor expansion of
\Psi_{W}(\bar{{\bf x}}+\delta{\bf x})
which are products of partial derivatives on
\Psi_{W}
, e.g.

\chi\in\frac{1}{i!}\partial_{a_{1}}\partial_{a_{2}}\dots\partial_{a_{i}}\Psi_{W}\delta x^{a_{1}}\delta x^{a_{2}}\dots\delta x^{a_{i}}~,~\forall i\geq 0~.

At
i=0
,
\chi=\Psi_{W}
.
\delta x^{a}
itself can be further expanded as
\delta x^{a}=\delta x^{a}{}^{[I]}+\delta x^{a}{}^{[II]}+\dots
, though the structure remains the same. In words, we say that “the dashed line is acting on object
M^{a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}}
”. Thus the dashed line represents the Green’s function solution of a source
\partial^{a}\chi
.

Using this archetype, and starting from the lowest order term, we introduce two further rules that allow for the consistent construction of all higher order terms. The lowest order term is the first order deflection when the dashed line acts on the Weyl potential
\chi=\Psi_{W}
,

Rule 2: Product rule.
Objects with index
i>1
are products of
\delta x^{a_{1}}{}^{[N_{1}]}
,
\delta x^{a_{2}}{}^{[N_{2}]}
,
\dots
, etc. The order of this object is then the sum
N=\sum_{i}N_{i}
. Since they all have the same argument, we link to
all
of them. Diagrammatically, we represent them with a product symbol “
\times
”

(103)

Since each dashed line carry along a single Weyl potential
\Psi_{W}
, the number of dashed lines of a diagram is equal to the perturbative order of the corresponding term, e.g. Eqn.
96
is 1st order, Eqn.
97
would be 2nd order and Eqn.
103
would be 4th order etc.

Using these rules, one can systematically construct the deflection to any order in perturbation theory. For the
\delta x^{a}{}^{[N]}(r)
term, draw a set of
N+1
nodes labeled linearly
r,r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{N}
. Then the total number of diagrams would be all possible diagrams with
N
dashed lines linking any two nodes such that all the nodes are linked with the following conditions: (a) there can only be a single line leaving a node to the left, (b) there is at least one line leaving a node to the right and (c) there are no line crossings. Once all the diagrams are drawn, we then use the rules described above to write down the corresponding intergrals.

Note that the no-line-crossing condition (c) above is a consequence of the fact that all the integrals are generated by either linked terms or product terms – which itself is a direct consequence of the fact that integrals are products of deflections which themselves are Green’s function solutions of the geodesic equation. If we allow for crossing terms, simple combinatorics tell us that there are
N!
possible ways of drawing
N
lines linking
N+1
nodes with conditions (a) and (b) above. However, imposing condition (c) means that the number of diagrams is less than
N!
.

We will now provide a convenient generating formula for iteratively constructing all the diagrams to all orders in
N
, starting from the two lowest order terms, Eqn.
95
and Eqn.
96
. Let
[N]
be the set of the diagrams at
N
-th order, such that
[0]=\{\hbox to4pt{\vbox to4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M -2.77 -2.77 M -2.77 -2.77 L -2.77 2.77 L 2.77 2.77 L 2.77 -2.77 Z M 2.77 2.77}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
;
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}
,

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}
etc.

Given the collection of known diagrams up to
[N]
, the diagrams of the next order
[N+1]
are generated by the formula

[N+1]=\bigcup_{i=0}^{i=N}[i]\#[N-i]~,

(104)

where
\bigcup
denotes “union of” and the product operator
\#
is defined such that
[i]\#[j]
denotes linking the left-most node of all the diagrams of
[i]
with the left-most node of all the diagrams of
[j]
with a dashed line. Note that
\#
does not commute, i.e.
[i]\#[j]\neq[j]\#[i]
for
i\neq j
. Since there are no repetitions in the generating formula Eqn.
104
, if
C_{N}
is the number of diagrams in
[N]
, then the total number of diagrams at
N+1
order is given by the recursion formula

C_{N+1}=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C_{i}C_{N-i}~.

(105)

Here,
C_{0}=C_{1}=1
as
[0]
and
[1]
have a single diagram each by construction. Finally, as the recursion formula Eqn.
105
is satisfied by the definition of the
Catalan number

C_{N}=\frac{1}{N+1}\binom{2N}{N}~,

(106)

this proves our assertion in the main text.
